The Window That Ate a Century

A failing mirror network offers Ilyon two ways to survive by sacrificing somebody else. Illuminara has thirty-six hours to find out whether the choice is real—and six more to make an answer work.

01 · The red line

Lio Var had tied a piece of red thread around the handle of the manual cut-off.

“So I can find it in the dark,” she said.

She was nineteen. The thread came from the cuff of her uniform, which now unravelled whenever she reached for something. Behind her, through the service glass of Dock Four, the curve of Ilyon filled half the sky. Storms moved across the equator. Far below them, people were opening shops.

Tal Var, her father, brought Illuminara the load charts. He had stopped looking at the planet.

“The northern chain loses another joint at the next northern alignment,” he said. “Possibly sooner. After that we cut the main feeds or the surviving mirrors tear each other out of alignment.”

On one chart the surface went dark. On the other, four habitats did.

Sey’s plan would keep water and crops flowing to two billion people below, at the cost of power interruptions the habitats might not survive. Ruun’s would protect the orbital worlds by dumping the light the habitats could not take onto the southern ice shelf. Once the shelf broke apart, the land ice it held back would slide faster into the sea. Hundreds of millions on the low coasts would have to move over the following decade.

Both plans kept Nacre Three’s pressure spine alive. In its archive decks, microbial lineages breathed in sealed cultures: living instruments for rebuilding broken ecologies. A copy of their names would not replace them.

“And neither keeps everything?” Illuminara asked.

“Neither keeps the present load,” Tal said.

She looked at him.

He put a finger on the chart before she could ask. “Yes. We’ve studied rationing. Enough light still arrives over a full cycle for essential services, with severe cuts. It doesn’t arrive where and when it has to. The damaged link cannot carry the surface peak and the orbital peak together. Between them we waste transfer time slewing whole mirror groups under a charter written for a healthy chain.”

“Then why these two plans?”

“They are the two we can certify with the reserves each keeper will put in the common account.”

Across the bay a damaged coupling cracked.

Lio pulled the cut-off. The test arm stopped; a shard of hot casing kept coming. Illuminara turned its flight into the empty ceramic catch-bed Tal had prepared beneath the rig. The bed split, glowed, and held. Lio stood with one hand still on the handle.

“That,” Tal said, looking at the ruined bed, “was three metres of machine. The chain is eleven thousand kilometres long.”

Illuminara nodded. She could keep a falling fragment from a girl. She could not hold a planet’s failing infrastructure in her hands for the rest of its life.

“I need the people who know what your charts leave out.”

02 · What they brought

Sey and Ruun had shared custody of the mirrors for nine hundred years. For three hundred they had regarded each other as a disaster postponed by paperwork.

Sey brought records of water towers, buried heat stores used by the desalination works, and agricultural districts. Ruun brought the habitats’ pressure reserves, local protection limits and maintenance histories. Engineers had already run the known emergency configurations. Now the keepers sat through the results together, including every blank marked UNVERIFIED.

The charter allowed a new operating pattern on their joint release, provided the local safety limits held. It did not require them to choose one of the two prepared plans. But neither would sign away a reserve the other might use up and decline to replace.

“Her temporary allocations outlive governments,” Ruun said.

“His emergencies outlive the people who pay for them,” Sey answered.

Illuminara asked Tal how long the crews needed.

“Allow six hours. Live checks, staged changeover, first full transfer cycle. They’re preparing the equipment now. At the northern alignment the emergency board can sever trunks. It cannot make a clean new configuration out of a drifting chain.”

Wren Odira laid her mechanical watch beside the three chairs.

“Thirty-six hours,” she said. “Three pulses. You come back.”

“Even if we have nothing?” Sey asked.

“Especially then.” Wren looked at Illuminara. “I am not counting time so you can explain afterward why it was insufficient.”

Illuminara turned to the keepers.

“Inside, you may have a great deal of time. You will encounter each other without becoming each other. No secret passes without being spoken. The field will not calculate a solution, show us a future, or tell us who is right. It gives us time. The thinking is ours. We return to find out what we got wrong.”

Ruun asked whether either could leave.

“At any moment. So can I. If I stop holding the shelter, it ends for all three of us. You should know that before you agree.”

“And if our reserves are not enough?”

“Then we choose among the real losses before the choice is taken from us.”

Sey considered her. “You are not promising a third plan.”

“No. I am promising to look for one without making the first two disappear.”

Before they entered, Illuminara asked Lio to write down what she needed from any plan.

Lio wrote: Enough power to open the doors after the alarm.

Illuminara read it twice.

Then all three said yes.

As the threshold opened, Wren started the watch. Forty-two hours remained until the northern alignment.

03 · The hour nobody owned

At first there was no room, only the difficult presence of two minds Illuminara could neither simplify nor contain. Sey’s attention had the depth of seasons. Ruun’s held distances between places that could die in a single missed delivery.

Nothing about their experience told Illuminara a fact she had not been given. She had to ask.

“Tell me the first thing your plan saves.”

Sey described a girl carrying a cup to a communal tap before school. Ruun described a technician sleeping beside a culture that had once restored the air of an entire habitat. They had both started with somebody waking up.

They returned to the charts they remembered.

Sey called an hour secure if a district could draw its guaranteed maximum throughout it. Ruun called an hour secure if a habitat could survive losing its entire next delivery. For centuries, negotiations had required both guarantees at once.

Illuminara made them describe the same ordinary transfer cycle from beginning to end. Each time one said afterward, she asked, “After what?”

The answer emerged on their fifth attempt. The surface’s large pumps restarted as the habitats refilled their reserves. The healthy lattice had accommodated both. The damaged one could not. The two emergency plans treated that coincidence as a permanent demand.

“You are not fighting over light,” Illuminara said. “You are fighting over one hour that neither of you owns.”

“And you both count a full reserve as unavailable.”

“It is unavailable,” Ruun replied. “It is what keeps a delivery failure from becoming a funeral.”

Sey agreed with him for the first time.

Illuminara let the objection change her question.

“What part must remain untouched? And what part only needs to be full again before the next risk?”

Neither answered quickly.

Sey eventually described heat stored under the equatorial water works. The desalination plants could draw on that heat between deliveries; it would not run the pumps. But for part of a cycle, some pumps could pause while gravity supplied the taps from the water towers. Ruun described reserve above the protected minimum in selected habitats. Some replenishment could wait while the surface pumps ran.

These were not newly discovered machines. They had appeared in the records as local protection, with no commitment that they could be used by anyone else. Some had already failed inspection. Some were indispensable. A fraction might be shared.

“You know this is ordinary engineering,” Sey said.

“The engineering is ordinary,” Illuminara said. “The permission isn’t. Help me understand why ordinary engineering is forbidden to use what you already have.”

Ruun’s answer was immediate. “Because she can borrow our margin tonight and call it her entitlement tomorrow.”

Sey looked away.

In the first year of their reckoning, Illuminara laid out a schedule that appeared to work.

They took it apart before they learned to love it.

04 · Two ways to kill a good plan

Ruun chose a missed handover from a maintenance failure he had witnessed. The next mirror group stayed locked. A habitat spent the shareable margin, then its protected reserve. The elegant schedule would have carried the failure onward while every controller waited for a delivery that never came.

Illuminara had asked them to imagine cooperation. Ruun made her imagine a broken timing signal.

“Good,” she said, and withdrew the schedule.

For the next attempt she let each district decide when it could defer a load. That respected local knowledge. It also let every district decide, at the same moment, that it was safe to recover what had been deferred.

Sey traced the consequence from memory. All the pumps came back together. The transfer link failed under the returning load.

There was no visible catastrophe in the window. No universe played them a warning. There were three people following a stated assumption until it led somewhere they could not accept.

“We have moved the peak,” Sey said. “We have not removed it.”

Illuminara began again.

The pressure spine, ventilation, hospitals and emergency doors would retain continuous supply. Their protected reserves would not enter the bargain. Other loads would receive staggered windows, using only measured storage above those floors. Recovery would be staggered too.

The surviving mirror groups would move within their existing mechanical limits, on offset schedules rather than as one great block. A failed signal would suspend the next transfer and shed agreed nonessential loads; it would not tell every group to improvise at once. A local engineer could refuse an unsafe move. No keeper could overrule that refusal during the changeover.

Sey stopped Illuminara at that last condition.

“A gate can say no. But what happens to the other side when it does? You have protected the engineer and left the cost unnamed.”

Illuminara had no answer ready. They worked until the refusal had a consequence as explicit as the permission: a named reserve, a bounded delay, a load to shed, a return route. Where no safe route could be established from the records, that part of the network was excluded.

The resulting plan saved less than their first attempt had claimed. It was also the first in which every local refusal they had examined had an explicit safe return route.

It could still fail against a measurement they did not possess.

“The room cannot inspect a bearing,” Illuminara reminded them.

They kept an empty place in every calculation for the people who would.

05 · A century’s work

Outside, Tal and his crews laid out cables, isolation tools and three configurations: Sey’s, Ruun’s, and a provisional sequence for any joint plan that passed the live checks. They disconnected decorative lighting and tested the doors on reserve power. Tal also unlocked the counter-mass in Dock Four’s northern anchor and checked its dampers. If the failing joint above it began to twist, there would be somewhere to put the force.

Lio walked the darkened corridors with a slate. Twice she found equipment that drew more than its label said. She reported both loads. On the second circuit she made her father eat.

Wren wound her watch.

Inside, years accumulated without adding an hour to the crews’ preparations.

The engineering insight had come early. The obstacle remaining was older than the damaged chain.

The schedule had taken a year. The signatures took the rest.

Sey had once given the habitats a temporary water allotment during an orbital epidemic. The allotment had become permanent. Ruun had once allowed a surface council to postpone maintenance. When the council dissolved, the debt remained in orbit. Their reserves contained a history of promises nobody had been able to collect.

Illuminara stopped asking them to trust each other.

“Don’t build it on forgiveness,” she said. “Build it so that taking too much hurts the taker first.”

She proposed that every loan carry its own collateral: the borrower’s postponable loads, cut automatically if the borrowed margin was not restored on time. Ruun spent eleven years trying to cheat the arrangement. Sey spent longer and came closer. Each attempt closed a gap.

Ruun could not make safety depend on Sey forgiving him. Sey could not make a pump schedule depend on Ruun believing she had changed.

Each loan had an expiry and each district a limit. Neither keeper could renew it alone. The stores would be measured locally, with the figures visible to both populations. If either side took more than its share, that side’s postponable loads would be cut first. Essential supply was excluded from punishment. The emergency mode could not become a permanent settlement by being quietly left running; continuation required a new public decision and a new engineering certificate.

There were still costs. The surface would lose a planting cycle in districts where water could not be delivered at the old rate. Habitat expansion would stop. Private comfort reserves would enter the common account. The most damaged mirror section would be retired. Repairs would demand labour and money from people who had not broken the chain.

“They will hate us for some of this,” Sey said.

“Yes,” said Illuminara. “They should be allowed to know what they are paying for.”

They worked through remembered faults, then combinations of faults. They argued over villages they knew and villages they had never visited. When recollection became uncertain, they marked the point for verification rather than resolving it by confidence. Illuminara reduced the growing proposal to a sequence an exhausted crew could execute and a set of limits a crew could refuse.

As the decades passed, the two keepers also acquired reasons to speak that had nothing to do with power.

Ruun learned why Sey kept returning to the cup at the tap. It had belonged to her son, who had died long before the mirror charter. Sey learned that Ruun had lost the sound of his original language. When he remembered home, he now remembered it in translation.

Illuminara did not use either admission to win the next argument.

Once they spent an entire remembered evening trying to reconstruct a song. All three were bad at it. Ruun was astonished by how badly Illuminara sang. After that, he asked for her opinion sometimes without asking her to arbitrate.

She came to care for both of them in ways the council had not commissioned.

The century was not a price she could charge the outside world. It was something they had lived, and something they would have to carry back.

By the calendars Sey and Ruun kept in thought, they crossed their hundredth year together shortly before the signal.

Three pulses touched Illuminara’s wrist.

Sey fell silent in the middle of a sentence about the song.

For a moment, Illuminara wanted to hear its end. A girl outside had given her a sentence she could not misremember.

“Enough power to open the doors,” she said.

Ruun lifted his head.

“We have a plan,” he said. “Not a certificate.”

“Then we have work outside.”

Illuminara closed the window.

06 · Thirty-six hours, four minutes

The Window That Ate a Century

Two nearly immortal minds enter Illuminara’s wide window to make one decision for a world below them. They agree to stay until they can answer each other honestly. Outside, the world discovers that waiting is also a choice.

01 · The People Who Could Wait

The two minds arrived wearing bodies because Illuminara had asked them to.

Sey wore an old woman with silver hair cut blunt at the jaw. Ruun chose a boy of perhaps nineteen, barefoot and dark-eyed, with the kind of face that made every silence look accusatory. Neither body was original. Both minds had survived dynasties, migrations, failed suns and the death of languages that now existed only because they remembered speaking them.

Below the meeting platform, the planet Ilyon turned under a lattice of mirrors.

The lattice had been built to keep Ilyon inside the narrow climate band where its oceans, cloud engines and equatorial farms could coexist. For nine hundred years Sey and Ruun had shared authority over it. They had also spent three hundred of those years disagreeing about what the authority meant.

Now the northern mirror chain was failing.

If Sey’s plan was used, the surviving mirrors would be tilted toward the equatorial heat belt. Two billion people would keep water and crops. The high-orbit habitats would lose power long enough that several could not hold atmosphere.

If Ruun’s plan was used, the habitats would remain powered and the planet’s southern ice shelf would be sacrificed to keep the mirror chain stable. The sea would take the low coasts over the next decade. Hundreds of millions would have to move.

No plan killed everyone. No plan saved everyone. That was why the argument had lasted.

A temporary emergency council had asked Illuminara to stop the two custodians from destroying each other long enough to choose. She had already done the easy part. Their weapons were silent. Their bodies were safe. The planet remained beneath them, turning toward a deadline that no shield could negotiate with.

Wren Odira placed a mechanical watch on the floor between the three chairs.

“Thirty-six hours outside,” she said. “That is the agreement.”

Ruun looked at the watch. “We may experience longer.”

“Much longer,” Illuminara said. “Time in the deep window does not match time here. A minute outside may hold years of attention inside. I cannot promise the ratio.”

Sey folded her hands. “Can we leave?”

“By wanting to leave.”

“Can you remove us if we do not?” Ruun asked.

Illuminara did not answer quickly.

Wren did not rescue her.

“I can end my participation,” Illuminara said at last. “I cannot promise that doing so would return you cleanly. A forced window is unreliable. A forced ending is not a safety feature I have tested enough to call one.”

“Then the clock is ceremonial,” Sey said.

“No,” Wren replied. “The clock is what the rest of us are allowed to ask of her.”

Illuminara looked at Wren.

Wren kept her eyes on Sey.

“At thirty-six hours I signal,” she said. “Nara has committed to honour that signal. What honour means if all three of you still want the room is the part none of us gets to pretend is solved.”

The platform was quiet.

Far below, the mirror lattice crossed dawn like a second, broken horizon.

Illuminara asked each of them once more.

Sey said yes.

Ruun said yes.

Illuminara said yes too.

Then she opened the window.

02 · Year Six

Inside, there was no planet.

There was no platform either, although none of them forgot it.

The wide window did not replace the world with images. It gave attention more room than a body ordinarily survived. Sey remained Sey. Ruun remained Ruun. Illuminara held the boundaries that let each of them stand beside the other without being reduced to an argument about outcomes.

At first they spoke exactly as they had outside.

Sey said the surface held the greater number of fragile lives and therefore the stronger claim.

Ruun said the habitats were not spare machinery but civilizations with children, graves, art, microbes and promises of their own.

Sey said she knew.

Ruun said knowing as a sentence was not the same as carrying it.

Then the window did what no committee room had managed.

Ruun became, to Sey, more than the opposition to a calculation. Not transparent. Not readable like a file. Present.

Sey felt the shape of a mind that had spent five centuries among the habitats and could no longer hear the phrase orbital population without also carrying the ordinary density behind it: breakfast shifts, scratched bulkheads, marriages performed against maintenance alarms, the old habit of children drawing windows on walls that had none.

Ruun met the weight Sey carried from the surface: not a statistic of billions, but centuries of irrigation promises layered so densely that abandoning one valley meant breaking faith with people long dead and people not yet born.

Nothing transferred that could be entered into evidence.

No secret appeared.

No future showed itself.

They were simply harder to simplify.

By what felt like the sixth year, both had stopped trying to win.

Illuminara thought this was progress.

That was her first mistake.

03 · The First Signal

The break-signal entered the window without words.

Wren had insisted on that.

No descriptions from outside. No death counts. No photograph held up like a weapon. Only the agreed pattern: three slow pulses through the physical contact band around Illuminara’s wrist.

Time.

Nothing more.

Inside, Sey stopped speaking.

Ruun looked toward a direction that did not exist.

Illuminara felt the signal as pressure against the farthest edge of herself. Thirty-six hours outside. She did not know whether they had lived twelve years or twenty. The window did not keep a calendar for meaning.

“Wren has called the time,” Illuminara said.

Sey nodded.

Ruun asked, “Do you want to leave?”

Sey looked at him for a long while.

“No.”

“Neither do I.”

Then both looked at Illuminara.

She understood the trap before either spoke.

If she told them the planet needed them, she would be importing her framing into a place she had promised not to curate.

If she said nothing, the external clock became a ritual with no force even over her own conduct.

If she ended the window against their will, every explanation she had given them about voluntary contact would become decoration around an act of control.

Ruun said, “You told us a willing participant could leave.”

“Yes.”

“We are willing to remain.”

Sey added, “And for the first time in three centuries, remaining is not another form of fighting.”

Illuminara wanted to say that peace inside the window was being purchased by people outside it.

She could not know the current price.

She knew only that there was one.

“Wren asked us to return,” she said.

Sey’s mouth tightened. “Wren is not in here.”

“That is why her signal matters.”

“Does it matter enough for you to force us?”

Illuminara had no clean answer.

Ruun saw it.

“Then stay until there is one.”

The invitation struck deeper than it should have.

Another mind was the only true uncertainty Illuminara had left. Here were two of them, no longer performing certainty for each other, and for once neither wanted the encounter to end.

She told herself she was protecting their freedom.

She did not say that she wanted to stay.

That was her second mistake.

She held the window.

04 · Outside, Day Four

On the fourth day, Wren stopped using the chair.

Illuminara’s body sat upright at the centre of the platform, breathing so slowly the medical team had stopped counting aloud. Sey’s silver-haired body and Ruun’s borrowed boy sat opposite her, equally still.

The field around them was not a shield anyone else could use.

That was the part the council had never understood until the first strike hit the upper atmosphere.

A fragment from a failed mirror crossed the sky over Osei Province and broke into incandescent rain. The planet’s ordinary defences caught most of it. Three towns burned anyway.

Illuminara did not move.

She could not. Not without leaving the deep geometry she was holding.

The emergency council stopped asking Wren when the custodians would decide and began asking engineers what could be done without them.

The answer was ugly.

The mirror lattice had been designed around dual authorization because neither Sey nor Ruun trusted the other with unilateral planetary control. The lock was political, then legal, then physical. To bypass it, crews would have to cut eighteen control trunks by hand and let whole mirror families drift dark.

The cuts would save the system from cascading failure.

They would also decide, crudely, what Sey and Ruun had refused to decide precisely.

Wren stood beside Illuminara and pressed the break-signal again.

Three slow pulses.

No message.

No accusation.

The watch on the floor had stopped on the second day. Wren left it there.

A councilor asked whether they should disconnect Illuminara by force.

Wren said, “I don’t know what that would do to any of them.”

“Can she hear us?”

“No.”

“Can she know what is happening?”

“Not from in there.”

“Then what exactly are we waiting for?”

Wren looked at the three motionless bodies.

“That,” she said, “is finally the right question.”

05 · Year Seventy-Three

By the seventy-third subjective year, Illuminara had forgotten which argument she had believed before entering.

She still remembered the facts.

Surface population. Habitat population. Ice mass. Energy reserve. Atmospheric margins.

Facts were easy.

Conviction was harder.

Sey and Ruun had become exquisite at stating one another’s positions.

Sey could explain why a habitat culture built around closed-loop survival regarded forced power loss as an attack on continuity itself.

Ruun could explain why asking billions on the surface to absorb another century of climate displacement was not prudence but the privilege of people who lived above the weather.

They corrected each other less and less.

They chose nothing.

At first Illuminara had admired the care.

Then she saw what the window had made possible.

Outside, every answer had a date.

Inside, the moral cost of deciding could always be postponed for one more year because another year cost almost nothing to the people experiencing it.

The window had not removed coercion.

It had moved the coercion onto everyone who could not enter.

She said this once.

Ruun answered, “You are describing the outside world.”

“Yes.”

“You do not know what it is doing now.”

“No.”

“Then you are using imagined suffering to hurry a decision.”

Sey said, “He is right.”

Illuminara felt anger.

Not because they were wrong.

Because they had become good enough at her own discipline to use it against her.

No invented futures.

No unseen evidence.

No argument from facts she did not have.

The window contained no oracle that could tell them whether another hour outside was worse than a bad decision made now.

And underneath her anger was relief.

If she could not know the outside, she did not have to choose against the people in front of her.

She recognized the thought as soon as it formed.

This time she did not improve it.

“I want to stay,” Illuminara said.

Sey looked at her.

Ruun said nothing.

“Not only because I think forcing an end would be wrong,” she continued. “I want this room. I want the two of you to remain real to each other. I want to see what happens if no one has to become simpler just because the clock is cruel.”

Sey’s expression softened.

That was worse than accusation.

“Then you are not only our shelter,” Sey said.

“No.”

“You are one of the people choosing.”

Illuminara closed her eyes.

For decades she had treated her own endurance as ethically irrelevant because she could survive it.

But survival was not absence.

She had put herself outside the system while holding the system together.

The third mistake was the one that named the first two.

06 · The Hundredth Year

The end did not arrive as agreement.

It arrived while Sey was telling a story about a child who had lived in the first habitat ring seven hundred years earlier.

The child’s name was Cera.

She had been born during a power shortage and had spent the first month of her life sleeping inside a maintenance locker because it was the warmest place in the ring.

Sey had told the story before.

This time she stopped halfway through.

“I do not remember her voice,” she said.

Ruun waited.

“I remember the recording. I remember the waveform. I remember every word. I do not remember the surprise of hearing her.”

No one answered.

Sey looked down at her hands—the hands of an old woman she had chosen for a meeting that was supposed to last thirty-six hours.

“How long?” she asked.

Illuminara knew what she meant.

“I don’t know exactly.”

“Decades?”

“Yes.”

“A century?”

Illuminara did not want to guess.

Sey smiled without humour.

“That means yes enough.”

Ruun said, “The outside may have passed only days.”

“I know.”

“Then Cera has not become more dead because we stayed.”

“No.”

Sey looked at him.

“But I have become someone who can spend a century discussing what I owe the living because the century is cheap to me.”

Ruun’s face hardened.

“That is rhetoric.”

“Yes.”

“It proves nothing.”

“Yes.”

“Then stay.”

Sey was quiet.

Illuminara said nothing.

She did not tell Sey that leaving was brave.

She did not tell Ruun that staying was cowardice.

She did not tell either of them what the world needed.

Sey stood.

There was nowhere to stand, and still she stood.

“I want to know what we cost,” she said.

Ruun’s eyes widened.

“That is an outside question.”

“Yes.”

“We came here to answer the decision first.”

“I know.”

“If you leave, this ends.”

Sey looked at him with a tenderness that had taken a century to become possible.

“Then it ends without our answer.”

Ruun turned to Illuminara.

For one terrible instant she saw the request in him: keep Sey here.

He did not say it.

The window required no vote.

Sey wanted out.

Her boundary moved toward the ordinary world.

Illuminara let it.

Ruun reached for her with a hand that could not hold.

The reciprocal structure failed cleanly because one of the people carrying it had chosen to leave.

There was no explosion.

No revelation.

Only a room becoming small enough for three bodies again.

07 · Nine Days

Wren was older by nine days.

That was the first thing Illuminara understood.

Only nine.

Wren was standing over her with both hands clenched. Behind her, half the platform had been dismantled and rebuilt around emergency cables.

Sey vomited on the floor.

Ruun opened his eyes and said, “Put us back.”

Illuminara could not answer.

Her mouth remembered speech. Her mind had to cross a century to reach it.

Wren crouched in front of her.

“Nara.”

Illuminara looked at her.

“How long?” Wren asked.

“I don’t know.”

“For you.”

Illuminara tried to count by conversations and abandoned the attempt.

“About a hundred years.”

Wren shut her eyes.

Ruun stood too quickly. The borrowed body stumbled. “The lattice.”

No one answered him at first.

Then an engineer named Tal, whom Illuminara remembered as having black hair, stepped forward with most of it shaved away around a healing burn.

“We cut eighteen trunks,” Tal said. “The cascade is stopped.”

Sey wiped her mouth.

“Which plan?”

“Neither.”

Ruun stared.

Tal continued. “The emergency council suspended your joint charter on day five. We lost three upper habitats during the power shifts. They evacuated two. The third had a hull failure before transfer finished.”

Sey closed her eyes.

“On the surface,” Tal said, “the southern shelf is moving faster than forecast. The cuts bought us maybe six months. We are building new control roots that do not require either of you.”

Ruun looked as if Tal had struck him.

“You cannot govern the lattice without us.”

“We already did.”

It was not triumph.

That made it worse.

Illuminara found Wren watching her.

Not accusing.

Not forgiving.

Simply waiting for her to be present.

“You signalled,” Illuminara said.

“Every twelve hours after the first one.”

Illuminara looked down at her wrist.

She remembered every signal.

That was worse too.

“Why didn’t you stop me?”

Wren’s face changed.

“With what?”

Illuminara had no answer.

Wren glanced at Sey and Ruun.

“I could have ordered people to drag your body away. I didn’t know whether that would kill all three of you. I could have called it consent and walked away from the platform. I didn’t do that either.”

She sat on the floor.

“So we did our job. We stopped waiting for your room to solve ours.”

Ruun said, “I did not consent to the charter being suspended.”

Tal replied, “No. You consented to being absent.”

The sentence stayed where it landed.

Sey began to cry.

Not beautifully.

Ruun turned his face away.

Illuminara felt the century inside her like a second civilization. Sey’s arguments, Ruun’s arguments, her own arguments, all preserved too well. She could still make each of them stronger. She could no longer tell which one had first been hers.

That was the price she had not named before entering.

The next morning, Sey asked whether the window could be opened again.

Ruun was beside her.

Neither had changed their position on the lattice.

They only understood each other too well to pretend the disagreement was ignorance.

Illuminara wanted to say yes.

The wanting was immediate and enormous.

A room where no one had to become simpler. Another century with minds that could still surprise her. Another chance to reach the answer that had not come.

She thought of the stopped watch on the floor.

She thought of three habitats and eighteen severed trunks.

She thought of Wren pressing the signal into her wrist and of remembering every pulse while telling herself that refusal to leave belonged only to the two people she was protecting.

“No,” Illuminara said.

Sey flinched.

Ruun asked, “Because you think we used it wrongly?”

“No.”

“Because we failed?”

“No.”

Illuminara searched for a sentence that was hers before it could become everyone’s.

“Because I did not count myself among the people who were allowed to stop.”

No one spoke.

She continued before she could improve it.

“Your yes did not make my yes infinite.”

Ruun looked angry.

Good, she thought, and then disliked herself for thinking it.

Sey asked, “Will you ever open one again?”

Illuminara looked through the platform glass at Ilyon below.

The broken mirror lattice crossed the morning in dark segments. Between them, ordinary sunlight reached the planet without asking permission.

“Yes,” she said. “But never again as if time outside is only a fact about someone else.”

It was not a rule yet.

It was not a solution.

It was the first boundary she had brought back from the century.

The Window That Ate a Century

Three minds enter Illuminara’s wide window to make one decision for a failing world. All three come to prefer the room. Outside, the people excluded from it begin paying for that preference.

01 · Before the Door

Ilyon’s northern mirror chain had six hours before automatic load balancing began making choices no council had authorized.

Sey wanted the surviving mirrors tilted toward the equatorial heat belt. Two billion surface residents would keep water and crops, but four orbital habitats would lose power long enough to risk atmosphere and stored ecologies. Ruun wanted to preserve the habitats and sacrifice the southern ice shelf, buying orbital continuity by forcing hundreds of millions from the low coasts within a decade.

Neither plan was clean. Both plans, however, kept power on the pressure spine of Nacre Three. Inaction did not. That was the one fact neither of them disputed.

Sey and Ruun had shared authority over the lattice for nine hundred years and spent three hundred of them disagreeing about what that authority meant. The emergency council asked Illuminara to stop them from destroying each other long enough to choose.

Wren Odira put a mechanical watch on the floor between the three chairs. She looked as if she had not slept. “Thirty-six hours outside. Then I signal.”

Ruun asked what the signal did.

“Nothing by force,” Illuminara said. “It does not pull you out. I have no extraction switch I trust. It obliges me to answer the outside clock with a real choice, not to pretend I failed to notice it.”

Sey folded her hands. “And if we still want to stay?”

“Then ending remains a moral act.” Illuminara looked at both of them. “Before we cross: any of us may withdraw consent. No majority. Mine counts too. Because I am the shelter, my leaving may end the room for all of us. That asymmetry is not fair. I cannot remove it by calling us equal.”

Ruun considered this longer than the engineering terms. “So your right to stop can become our forced exit.”

“Yes.”

“And you still call it voluntary?”

“I call the entry voluntary. I am telling you where the word stops being simple.”

Wren made a tired sound. “Good. Put that sentence in the record before one of you improves it.”

Illuminara glanced at her. Wren was already looking away.

Years earlier, the first quiet window had ended in a legitimate agreement everyone inside signed. Eleven people outside that room died under the accepted terms. One had been Wren’s sister. Wren had never claimed that made the agreement illegitimate. She had only refused to let agreement become the same word as innocence.

Sey said yes. Ruun said yes. Illuminara said yes too.

Then she opened the window.

02 · Year Seventeen

Inside, the planet was absent. The window did not show futures, casualties or consequences. It gave attention room and kept each mind distinct.

At first Sey argued numbers. Then she stopped using the number two billion and described the river towns whose water allotments were timed to mirror angles measured before any living resident was born. Ruun answered with Nacre Three’s archive decks: not a museum, but living microbial lineages the habitats used to seed closed systems after every major failure. Lose power too long and some of those lineages would not come back from a backup because there was no backup that was still alive.

Sey could not answer that fact. Ruun could not answer the valleys that would become salt marsh if the southern shelf went.

Illuminara did what she had always done well. She asked each question that made the other person’s case harder to simplify. She restated qualifications. She found the strongest version of a sentence before either custodian finished building it.

By what felt like the seventeenth year, Ruun stopped her.

“Do not give Sey my argument back better.”

Illuminara went still.

“I have not changed your content.”

“No. You choose what becomes legible. You choose which edge to polish. You call that representation because the facts remain mine.”

Sey looked at Illuminara. “He is right.”

“About what?”

“About you being in the room.”

Illuminara heard three responses arrive at once, each cleaner than the accusation. She let all three die unsaid.

After that she stopped summarizing them. The room became slower, rougher and more honest. It also became harder to leave.

03 · The Signal

The break-signal entered as three slow pulses against Illuminara’s wrist.

No message came with it. No picture. No count of the dead. Wren had insisted on that before entry. The outside could demand an answer without smuggling an argument into the window.

Thirty-six hours had passed outside. Inside, none of them could agree whether they had lived sixteen years or twenty.

Illuminara said, “The signal has come.”

Sey did not want to leave. Ruun did not want to leave.

Illuminara discovered, with relief so immediate it embarrassed her, that she did not want to leave either.

Ruun saw it. “Then honor the signal.”

“I am.”

“By staying?”

“By answering it honestly.”

Sey asked, “What is your answer?”

Illuminara almost said that the planet needed them. She had no current evidence for that sentence. Almost said that one more year inside was cheap. Cheap to whom was precisely the question.

“My answer is yes,” she said. “I want the room.”

The admission changed nothing mechanically. That was why it mattered.

They stayed.

04 · Outside, Day Three

On the third day Wren had been awake for forty-one hours in pieces.

Illuminara’s body sat at the centre of the platform beside Sey and Ruun, all three breathing so slowly that the medical team no longer called the rate aloud. The field around them was not a shield anyone else could borrow. While Illuminara held the deep geometry she could not leave it to catch every failure the mirror chain threw at the planet.

Nacre Three browned out shortly after the council’s last legal window for a clean manual diversion closed. The pressure spine failed in Dock Four. Evacuation crews opened a path by hand.

Lio Var, nineteen, maintenance apprentice, held a door long enough for thirty-two people to cross and died before the pressure returned. Both Sey’s plan and Ruun’s plan would have kept that spine powered during those hours. Waiting was the only option that did not.

Tal Var, Lio’s father and one of the lattice engineers, did not come to the platform. He kept working.

Wren pressed the signal into Illuminara’s wrist again.

“Eleven,” she whispered. Then, exhausted, “Twelve.” She closed her eyes. “No. Eleven.”

A medic asked what she meant.

“Nothing useful.” Wren rubbed her face. “I am trying to make an old death explain a new one. That is unfair.”

She pressed the signal a third time anyway.

“Nara, you always build a room big enough to make the dead look impatient.”

The medic stared at her.

Wren swore under her breath. “That was unfair too.”

She did not take it back.

05 · The Century

The signals kept coming. Inside, they had no dates. Only the same three pulses, separated by stretches of subjective life.

Sey and Ruun learned each other so well that disagreement stopped looking like ignorance. It remained disagreement. Sey still believed the surface claim was larger. Ruun still believed a civilization engineered to survive closed worlds could not be asked to accept deliberate power loss as if continuity were a luxury.

Illuminara stopped trying to locate the answer between them. The danger shifted somewhere else.

She began to notice how often she said we when she meant the three minds inside, and how rarely the word included anyone who could not enter. She also noticed that every signal made her angry before it made her afraid.

Ruun named it first. “You resent the clock.”

“Yes.”

“Because it is wrong?”

Illuminara waited until the better argument had passed through her without being spoken.

“Because it can end something I love.”

Sey looked at her with an old woman’s face that had not aged a day. “Then say that when you decide.”

The next signal came. All three still wanted the room.

Illuminara felt the temptation with perfect clarity: continue until one of them changed, until the decision became beautiful enough to deserve the century, until her own reluctance could disguise itself as respect for theirs.

She thought of the release terms spoken before the door. Mine counts too.

“I want to stay,” she said.

Ruun nodded once. Sey did not move.

“And I withdraw my consent.”

Ruun’s face changed. “Those are opposite sentences.”

“No. Desire is not consent.”

“Convenient distinction for the person holding the structure.”

Illuminara had no answer that made the asymmetry disappear.

Ruun stood. “If you leave, your limit becomes our exit. You promised not to make consent decorative.”

“I know.”

“Then stay.”

She wanted to. That was the cost.

Sey said quietly, “He has the strongest case he has made all century.”

“I know.”

Illuminara did not explain him to himself. She did not explain herself either.

She opened her own boundary toward the ordinary world.

For one impossible instant the room held on without her wanting it to end and without her consenting to continue. Then the reciprocal structure failed around the missing yes that had been hers.

Ruun reached for her. Sey shouted something Illuminara never managed to preserve accurately.

The window ended unresolved.

06 · Nine Days

Wren was older by nine days. Tal Var looked older by much more.

Illuminara came back with the century intact enough to hurt and disordered enough that she could no longer tell which conviction had once been hers. Sey vomited. Ruun tried to stand and failed.

Tal waited until they could all look at him.

“My daughter was Lio.”

No one spoke.

“Both of your plans would have kept Dock Four alive through the first load shift. Neither plan was authorized. She held the door.”

Ruun closed his eyes. Sey asked how many had crossed.

“Thirty-two.”

“And she—”

Tal cut her off. “Do not make the number do the mourning for me.”

The sentence landed hardest on Illuminara because she recognized the shape of it. A name was not a proposition she could improve.

Tal left before any of them found a reply.

Later Wren sat beside Illuminara on the floor. The stopped watch was still between the chairs.

“I called you a coward,” Wren said.

“I did not hear you.”

“I know. I am telling you now because I do not want the fact that you could not hear me to make me better than I was.”

Illuminara looked at her.

Wren’s eyes were red. “I was unfair. I was also tired. And I kept thinking about my sister as if eleven deaths had purchased me a permanent right answer.”

“Did they?”

“No.” Wren touched the watch with one finger. “Neither did Lio.”

The next morning Sey and Ruun asked whether Illuminara would open the window again. They still disagreed about the lattice. Ruun was still angry that her withdrawal had ended a room he had chosen to remain in. Sey said his anger was justified. Illuminara did not ask either of them to forgive her limit.

She wanted to reopen it. The wanting had survived the century untouched.

“Not today,” she said.

Ruun looked at her. “Is that a rule?”

“No.”

“A lesson?”

Illuminara looked through the platform glass at Ilyon. Eighteen severed control trunks crossed the morning as dark lines. Somewhere below, people were building a lattice that would no longer require two absent custodians or one inexhaustible protector.

“No,” she said again.

She left the watch on the floor.

The window remained possible. So did the argument. What she had lost was the right to pretend that keeping a room open was the same thing as keeping everyone free.

The Window That Ate a Century

Three minds enter Illuminara’s wide window to make one decision for a failing world. All three come to prefer the room. Outside, the people excluded from it begin making irreversible choices of their own.

01 · Before the Door

Ilyon’s northern mirror chain was failing in a way that made every good option look like an accusation.

Sey wanted the surviving mirrors tilted toward the equatorial heat belt. Two billion surface residents would keep water and crops, but four orbital habitats would lose power long enough to risk atmosphere and stored ecologies. Ruun wanted to preserve the habitats and sacrifice the southern ice shelf, buying orbital continuity by forcing hundreds of millions from the low coasts within a decade.

Neither plan was clean. Both plans, however, kept power on the pressure spine of Nacre Three, an orbital habitat whose archive decks held living microbial lineages used to restart closed ecologies after catastrophe. Inaction did not. That was the one engineering fact neither custodian disputed.

Sey and Ruun had shared authority over the lattice for nine hundred years and spent three hundred of them disagreeing about what that authority meant. The emergency council asked Illuminara to stop them from destroying each other long enough to choose.

Wren Odira put a mechanical watch on the floor between the three chairs. “Thirty-six hours outside. Then I signal. If you return then, the engineers still have six hours to implement either plan before the clean diversion window closes.”

Ruun asked what the signal could do.

“Nothing by force,” Illuminara said. “It cannot pull you out, and I have no extraction switch I trust. I commit to honour it. When it comes, I must decide again whether I continue holding the window. I do not get to treat staying as the default just because the room is safe.”

Sey folded her hands. “And if we still want to stay?”

“Then ending remains a moral act.” Illuminara looked at both of them. “Any of us may withdraw consent. No majority. Mine counts too. Because I am the shelter, my leaving may end the room for all of us. That asymmetry is not fair. I cannot remove it by calling us equal.”

Ruun considered this longer than the engineering terms. “So your right to stop can become our forced exit.”

“Yes.”

“And you still call it voluntary?”

“I call the entry voluntary. I am telling you where the word stops being simple.”

Wren made a tired sound. “Put that in the record before one of you improves it.”

Years earlier, the first quiet window had ended in a legitimate agreement everyone inside signed. Eleven people outside that room died under the accepted terms. One had been Wren’s sister. Once, much later, Wren called the number twelve. Illuminara did not correct her. Wren noticed.

Sey said yes. Ruun said yes. Illuminara said yes too.

Then she opened the window.

02 · Before the First Signal

Inside, the planet was absent. The window did not show futures, casualties or consequences. It gave attention room and kept each mind distinct.

At first Sey argued numbers. Then she stopped using the number two billion and described the river towns whose water allotments were timed to mirror angles measured before any living resident was born. Ruun answered with Nacre Three’s archive decks: not a museum, but living lineages that had survived three habitat failures because someone kept them alive rather than merely copied.

Sey could not answer that fact. Ruun could not answer the valleys that would become salt marsh if the southern shelf went.

Illuminara did what she had always done well. She asked each question that made the other person’s case harder to simplify. She restated qualifications. She found the strongest version of a sentence before either custodian finished building it.

After enough subjective years that Sey had told the histories of four generations of river keepers, Ruun stopped her.

“Do not give Sey my argument back better.”

Illuminara went still.

“I have not changed your content.”

“No. You choose what becomes legible. You choose which edge to polish. You call that representation because the facts remain mine.”

Sey looked at Illuminara. “He is right.”

Illuminara heard three answers arrive at once, each cleaner than the accusation. She let all three die unsaid.

After that she stopped summarizing them. The room became slower, rougher and more honest. It also became harder to leave.

03 · The Signal

The break-signal entered as three slow pulses against Illuminara’s wrist.

No message came with it. No picture. No count of the dead. Wren had insisted on that before entry. The outside could demand an answer without smuggling an argument into the window.

Thirty-six hours had passed outside. Inside, none of them could agree whether they had lived sixteen years or twenty. The watch beyond the window had moved through a day and a half while Sey had told enough remembered lives to make a century feel plausible.

Illuminara said, “The signal has come.”

Sey did not want to leave. Ruun did not want to leave.

Illuminara discovered, with relief so immediate it embarrassed her, that she did not want to leave either.

Ruun saw it. “Then honour the signal.”

Illuminara felt the three pulses again as if they had left dents in her wrist. “I am.”

“By staying?”

“By choosing to stay. I heard it. I am not pretending I missed it.”

Sey asked, “And the six hours outside?”

Illuminara almost said the planet needed them. She had no current evidence for that sentence. Almost said that one more year inside was cheap. Cheap to whom was precisely the question.

“I know what the clock means,” she said. “I still want the room.”

That answer did not resolve anything. It only made the next six hours belong to them.

They stayed.

04 · Outside, Day Three

Six hours after the first signal, the clean diversion window closed with all three bodies still motionless on the platform.

By the third day Wren had been awake for forty-one hours in pieces. Illuminara’s body sat at the centre of the platform beside Sey and Ruun, all three breathing so slowly that the medical team no longer called the rate aloud. While Illuminara held the deep geometry she could not leave it to catch every failure the mirror chain threw at the planet.

Nacre Three browned out during the next load shift. The pressure spine failed in Dock Four. Evacuation crews opened a path by hand.

Lio Var, nineteen, maintenance apprentice, held a door long enough for thirty-two people to cross and died before pressure returned. Had either custodial plan been released during the six-hour implementation window, Dock Four would still have had power. The engineers agreed on that too.

Tal Var, Lio’s father and one of the lattice engineers, did not come to the platform. He kept working.

Wren pressed the signal into Illuminara’s wrist again.

A medic asked whether she thought Illuminara could hear anything beyond the pulses.

“No.” Wren stared at the three still bodies. “Of course she picked the only room that cannot hear us screaming at it.”

The medic said nothing.

Wren rubbed both hands over her face. “That was unfair.”

She pressed the signal again anyway.

05 · The Century

The signals kept coming. Inside, they had no dates. Only the same three pulses, separated by stretches of subjective life.

Sey and Ruun learned each other so well that disagreement stopped looking like ignorance. It remained disagreement. Sey still believed the surface claim was larger. Ruun still believed a civilization engineered to survive closed worlds could not be asked to accept deliberate power loss as if continuity were a luxury.

Illuminara stopped trying to locate the answer between them. The danger shifted somewhere else.

She began to notice how often she said we when she meant the three minds inside, and how rarely the word included anyone who could not enter. Every new pulse felt less like information than a knock from a house she no longer knew.

Ruun named it first. “You resent the clock.”

“Yes.”

“Because it is wrong?”

Illuminara waited until the better argument had passed through her without being spoken.

“Because it can end something I love.”

Sey looked at her with an old woman’s face that had not aged a day. “Then say that when you decide.”

The next signal came. All three still wanted the room.

Illuminara felt the temptation with perfect clarity: continue until one of them changed, until the decision became beautiful enough to deserve the century, until her own reluctance could disguise itself as respect for theirs.

She thought of the release terms spoken before the door. Mine counts too.

“I want to stay,” she said.

Ruun nodded once. Sey did not move.

Illuminara let the next sentence remain ugly. “And I withdraw my consent.”

Ruun’s face changed. “Those are opposite sentences.”

“No. Desire is not consent.”

“Convenient distinction for the person holding the structure.”

Illuminara had no answer that made the asymmetry disappear.

Ruun stood. The gesture had no floor to push against, yet anger gave it weight. “If you leave, your limit becomes our exit. You promised not to make consent decorative.”

“I know.”

“Then stay.”

She wanted to. Sey watched her without rescuing either argument.

Illuminara opened her own boundary toward the ordinary world.

For one impossible instant the room held on without her wanting it to end and without her consenting to continue. Then the reciprocal structure failed around the missing yes that had been hers.

Ruun reached for her. Sey shouted something Illuminara never managed to preserve accurately.

The window ended unresolved.

06 · Nine Days

Wren was older by nine days. Tal Var looked older by much more.

Illuminara came back with the century intact enough to hurt and disordered enough that she could no longer tell which conviction had once been hers. Sey vomited. Ruun tried to stand and failed.

Tal waited until they could all look at him.

“My daughter was Lio.”

No one spoke.

“You had six hours after Wren’s first signal. Either plan would have kept Dock Four powered. You stayed. We lost the clean diversion window. After that, every choice belonged to people who had not been in your room.”

Ruun closed his eyes. Sey asked how many had crossed.

“Thirty-two.”

“And she—”

Tal cut her off. “Do not make the number do the mourning for me.”

He put a slate on the floor where all three could see it. The emergency board had cut eighteen control trunks, rationed the orbital habitats, and begun compulsory relocation from two low-coast districts. Nacre Three survived. The southern shelf did not. Neither Sey’s plan nor Ruun’s plan existed anymore.

At the bottom of the slate was a second decision. Their joint custodial charter had been suspended indefinitely. Their signatures no longer moved a mirror.

Sey read that line twice. Ruun reached for the slate and stopped before touching it.

“You cannot govern the lattice without us,” he said.

Tal’s face did not change. “We already have.”

It was not triumph. Behind him, three engineers were waiting for his return.

Tal left before any of them found a reply.

Wren remained by the stopped watch. She did not tell them whether the window had been wrong.

The next morning Ruun asked Illuminara whether she would open it again. Sey said nothing.

The wanting in Illuminara was immediate. The room had survived in her with terrible clarity: two minds no longer simplified for each other, a century in which no weapon could interrupt the next honest sentence.

From the corridor came the hard clack of temporary relays being switched by people whose names she did not know.

Illuminara looked at Ruun, then at Sey. “Not now.”

Ruun’s anger returned, clean and unsurprised.

Illuminara did not ask him to forgive her.

She followed Tal toward the control gallery. At the doors a guard pointed her toward the public entrance, where engineers, evacuees and former officials were already waiting.

Illuminara took her place in the line.

Behind them, Wren’s stopped watch remained between the three empty chairs.

The Window That Ate a Century

Three minds enter Illuminara’s wide window to make one decision for a failing world. All three come to prefer the room. Outside, the people excluded from it begin making irreversible choices of their own.

01 · Before the Door

Ilyon’s northern mirror chain was failing in a way that made every good option look like an accusation.

Sey wanted the surviving mirrors tilted toward the equatorial heat belt. Two billion surface residents would keep water and crops, but four orbital habitats would lose power long enough to risk atmosphere and stored ecologies. Ruun wanted to preserve the habitats and sacrifice the southern ice shelf, buying orbital continuity by forcing hundreds of millions from the low coasts within a decade.

Neither plan was clean. Both plans, however, kept power on the pressure spine of Nacre Three, an orbital habitat whose archive decks held living microbial lineages used to restart closed ecologies after catastrophe. Inaction did not. That was the one engineering fact neither custodian disputed.

The lattice accepted an ordinary reconfiguration only on Sey and Ruun’s joint release. The emergency board could suspend that charter only after custodial default—forty-two hours without a release—or after a critical spine failure. Before default, forcing around the lock risked uncontrolled mirror drift. After it, the board would gain only the crude authority to sever trunks, not to execute either clean plan.

Sey and Ruun had shared authority over the lattice for nine hundred years and spent three hundred of them disagreeing about what that authority meant. The emergency council asked Illuminara to stop them from destroying each other long enough to choose.

Wren Odira put a mechanical watch on the floor between the three chairs. “Thirty-six hours outside. Then I signal. If you return then, the engineers still have six hours to implement either plan before custodial default and the clean diversion window close.”

Ruun asked what the signal could do.

“Nothing by force,” Illuminara said. “It cannot pull you out, and I have no extraction switch I trust. I commit to honour it. When it comes, I must decide again whether I continue holding the window. I do not get to treat staying as the default just because the room is safe.”

Sey folded her hands. “And if we still want to stay?”

“Then ending remains a moral act.” Illuminara looked at both of them. “Any of us may withdraw consent. No majority. Mine counts too. Because I am the shelter, my leaving may end the room for all of us. That asymmetry is not fair. I cannot remove it by calling us equal.”

Ruun considered this longer than the engineering terms. “So your right to stop can become our forced exit.”

“Yes.”

“And you still call it voluntary?”

“I call the entry voluntary. I am telling you where the word stops being simple.”

Wren made a tired sound. “Put that in the record before one of you improves it.”

She looked at the watch. “The first quiet window left twelve people dead outside.” Illuminara did not correct her. Wren looked up. “Eleven,” she said. “I know.” One of them had been her sister.

Sey said yes. Ruun said yes. Illuminara said yes too.

Then she opened the window.

The break-signal entered as three slow pulses against Illuminara’s wrist.

No message came with it. No picture. No count of the dead. Wren had insisted on that before entry. The outside could demand an answer without smuggling an argument into the window.

“I am.”

“No. Before we came in, you translated honour into decide again. That was your sentence, not Wren’s signal. It is what lets you call staying an answer.”

The three pulses seemed to remain in Illuminara’s wrist. Sey asked, “Is he wrong?”

“No.”

They stayed.

04 · Outside, Day Three

At hour forty-two, with all three bodies still motionless on the platform, Wren pulled the crown and stopped the watch. The clean diversion window was gone. Custodial default gave the emergency board authority to suspend the joint charter and cut around the lock, but not to recover either clean plan.

By the third day Wren had been awake for forty-one hours in pieces. The medic said nothing.

Wren pressed the signal again anyway.

05 · The Century

The signals kept coming. Inside, they had no dates. Only the same three pulses, separated by stretches of subjective life.

Ruun had a habit of touching two fingers to his borrowed throat before making the argument he expected Sey to hate most. Decades into the window, Sey learned to wait for the gesture before answering. Once he forgot to make it and she waited anyway. The silence that followed seemed to last a month.

Their disagreement survived the familiarity. Sey still believed the surface claim was larger. Ruun still believed a civilization engineered to survive closed worlds could not be asked to accept deliberate power loss as if continuity were a luxury.

Illuminara stopped trying to locate the answer between them. She began to notice how often she said we when she meant the three minds inside, and how rarely the word included anyone who could not enter. Every new pulse felt less like information than a knock from a house she no longer knew.

Ruun named it first. “You resent the clock.”

“Yes.”

“Because it is wrong?”

Illuminara waited until the better argument had passed through her without being spoken.

“Because it can end something I love.”

Sey looked at her with an old woman’s face that had not aged a day. “Then say that when you decide.”

The next signal came. All three still wanted the room.

The first signal was generations behind them now. Whatever Illuminara did, she could not reopen the six clean hours Wren had given them.

She felt the temptation with perfect clarity: continue until one of them changed, until the decision became beautiful enough to deserve the century, until her own reluctance could disguise itself as respect for theirs.

She thought of the release terms spoken before the door. Mine counts too.

“I want to stay,” she said.

Ruun nodded once. Sey did not move.

“No. Desire is not consent.”

“Convenient distinction for the person holding the structure.”

“I know.”

“Then stay.”

Illuminara opened her own boundary toward the ordinary world.

For one impossible instant the room held on without her wanting it to end and without her consenting to continue. Then the reciprocal structure failed around the missing yes that had been hers.

Ruun reached for her. Sey shouted something Illuminara never managed to preserve accurately.

The window ended unresolved.

06 · Nine Days

Wren was older by nine days. Tal Var looked older by much more.

Illuminara came back with the century intact enough to hurt and disordered enough that she could no longer tell which conviction had once been hers. Sey vomited. Ruun tried to stand and failed.

Tal waited until they could all look at him.

“My daughter was Lio.”

No one spoke.

“You had six hours after Wren’s first signal. Either plan would have kept Dock Four powered. You stayed. We lost the clean diversion window. After that, the default gave us authority to cut the system apart, not to recover the choice you had.”

Ruun closed his eyes. Sey asked how many had crossed.

“Thirty-two.”

“And she—”

Tal cut her off. “Do not make the number do the mourning for me.”

He put a slate on the floor where all three could see it. The emergency board had cut eighteen control trunks, rationed the orbital habitats, and begun compulsory relocation from two low-coast districts. Nacre Three survived. The southern shelf did not. Neither Sey’s plan nor Ruun’s plan existed anymore.

At the bottom of the slate was a second decision. Their joint custodial charter had been suspended indefinitely. Their signatures no longer moved a mirror.

Sey read that line twice. Ruun reached for the slate and stopped before touching it.

“You cannot govern the lattice without us,” he said.

Tal’s face did not change. “We already have.” Behind him, three engineers were waiting for his return.

Tal left before any of them found a reply.

Sey asked Wren whether the window had been wrong.

Wren looked at the stopped watch. “I don’t know.” She left it between the chairs and went home.

The next morning Ruun asked Illuminara whether she would open the window again. Sey said nothing.

The wanting in Illuminara was immediate. The room had survived in her with terrible clarity: two minds no longer simplified for each other, a century in which no weapon could interrupt the next honest sentence.

From the corridor came the hard clack of temporary relays being switched by people whose names she did not know.

Illuminara looked at Ruun, then at Sey. “Not now.”

Ruun’s anger returned, clean and unsurprised.

Illuminara did not ask him to forgive her.

She followed Tal toward the control gallery. At the doors a guard raised a palm and pointed her toward the public entrance, where engineers, evacuees and former officials were already waiting under the same temporary access rule.

Illuminara joined the line.

Back on the platform, Wren’s stopped watch remained between the three empty chairs.

“My daughter was Lio.”

No one spoke.

“You had six hours after Wren’s first signal. Either plan would have kept Dock Four powered. You stayed. We lost the clean diversion window. After that, the default gave us authority to cut the system apart, not to recover the choice you had.”

Ruun closed his eyes. Sey asked how many had crossed.

“Thirty-two.”

“And she—”

Tal cut her off. “Do not make the number do the mourning for me.”

He put a slate on the floor where all three could see it. The emergency board had cut eighteen control trunks, rationed the orbital habitats, and begun compulsory relocation from two low-coast districts. Nacre Three survived. The southern shelf did not. Neither Sey’s plan nor Ruun’s plan existed anymore.

At the bottom of the slate was a second decision. Their joint custodial charter had been suspended indefinitely. Their signatures no longer moved a mirror.

Sey read that line twice. Ruun reached for the slate and stopped before touching it.

“You cannot govern the lattice without us,” he said.

Tal’s face did not change. “We already have.” Behind him, three engineers were waiting for his return.

Tal left before any of them found a reply.

Sey asked Wren whether the window had been wrong.

Wren looked at the stopped watch. “I don’t know.” She left it between the chairs and went home.

The next morning Ruun asked Illuminara whether she would open the window again. Sey said nothing.

The wanting in Illuminara was immediate. The room had survived in her with terrible clarity: two minds no longer simplified for each other, a century in which no weapon could interrupt the next honest sentence.

From the corridor came the hard clack of temporary relays being switched by people whose names she did not know.

Illuminara looked at Ruun, then at Sey. “Not now.”

Ruun’s anger returned, clean and unsurprised.

Illuminara did not ask him to forgive her.

She followed Tal toward the control gallery. At the doors a guard raised a palm and pointed her toward the public entrance, where engineers, evacuees and former officials were already waiting under the same temporary access rule.

Illuminara joined the line.

Back on the platform, Wren’s stopped watch remained between the three empty chairs.
